      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{xu2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy, yet every one outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without indicating \\emph{which sentences} support each prediction.\nWithout evidence, a clinician reviewing a ten-page case report must manually locate the sentences that support each extracted drug interaction, a bottleneck that undermines trust in automated extraction.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[!t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.92\\textwidth]{fig_overview.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Evidence-grounded DocRE produces relation triples with supporting sentence indices, unlike standard DocRE which outputs bare triples. RSFT selects the top-$k$ of $N$ CED-scored candidates per document for fine-tuning.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth produce evidence and improve extraction, but encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in accuracy.\nThe community faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a structural difference.\nIn encoder methods, evidence retrieval is classification that regularizes the encoder at negligible cost; in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires the model to \\emph{generate} additional output tokens per triple, increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nThe na\\\"{i}ve expectation is that this generation cost always harms extraction accuracy.\nOur experiments show this expectation is wrong.\nThe evidence tax (Ign-F1(no-evidence) minus Ign-F1(with-evidence), so positive values indicate cost and negative values indicate benefit) is not a fixed penalty but a landscape shaped by model capacity, optimization paradigm, and training data quality.\nWithin the Qwen3 family, evidence generation \\emph{benefits} extraction at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1, partly through halving output duplication) but is approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B).\nGradient-based optimization amplifies this competition, as GRPO collapses extraction under constrained precision and DPO progressively trades extraction for evidence, while discrete sample selection (RSFT) avoids the trade-off entirely by filtering outputs rather than pushing gradients. To quantify this decoupling, we define the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic metric that measures the fraction of cited evidence not supported by gold annotations.\n\nWe study evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and compare three post-training paradigms across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) \\citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) \\citep{rafailov2023direct}, and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT), building on Self-Taught Reasoner (STaR) \\citep{zelikman2022star}. Our contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=1pt,parsep=0pt,topsep=2pt,partopsep=0pt,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Evidence generation is not uniformly harmful.} We observe a scale-dependent evidence tax with a within-family capacity threshold: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B), challenging the assumption that evidence always costs extraction accuracy.\n    \\item \\textbf{Gradient pressure causes evidence-extraction decoupling.} We characterize a decoupling spectrum (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none) and introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic, validated at per-relation level ($\\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$).\n    \\item \\textbf{RSFT improves extraction without decoupling.} NLI-based sample selection improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) while maintaining evidence quality. We observe lower cross-seed variance under RSFT at all tested configurations, though this partly reflects shared SFT initialization and is limited by $n{=}3$ seeds (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
      "numLines": 34,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 34
    }
  },

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task: Evidence-Grounded Document-Level Relation Extraction}
5	\label{sec:task}
6	
7	Given a document $D = \{s_1, \ldots, s_n\}$ consisting of $n$ sentences and a set of entities $\mathcal{E}$ identified within $D$, the task of evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires predicting, for each entity pair $(e_h, e_t) \in \mathcal{E} \times \mathcal{E}$, a relation $r \in \mathcal{R} \cup \{\texttt{NA}\}$ together with a set of supporting evidence sentences $E \subseteq D$. Standard DocRE predicts only the triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$; the evidence-grounded formulation also requires $E$, which grounds each prediction in specific textual support (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}).
8	
9	In our LLM-based approach, the model generates structured outputs that jointly encode both components. For each predicted relation, the output follows the format \texttt{(head, relation, tail) | Evidence: [S$i$][S$j$]}, requiring the model to produce complete evidence references inline with each triple rather than as a separate retrieval step. This design contrasts with encoder-based evidence methods such as SAIS~\citep{xiao2022sais} and DREEAM~\citep{ma2023dreeam}, which frame evidence identification as sentence-level binary classification. Because classification over a fixed sentence set does not increase output length, these methods incur no generation overhead. Our LLM formulation requires additional tokens for each evidence reference, creating a generation burden that scales with the number of predicted triples and their evidence sentences. This scale-dependent cost is the central phenomenon we investigate.
10	
11	
12	\subsection{Supervised Fine-Tuning Baseline}
13	\label{sec:sft}
14	
15	We fine-tune all models with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) \citep{hu2022lora} at rank $r{=}64$ and scaling factor $\alpha{=}128$, applied to all linear projections (attention and feed-forward). The training data consists of DocRED~\citep{yao2019docred} training documents converted to the structured output format described above, where each example maps a document and its entity set to the complete set of relation triples with evidence annotations. We also train a \emph{no-evidence} variant under identical configuration but with evidence references removed from the output format, producing only \texttt{(head, relation, tail)}. This variant serves as an upper bound on extraction performance by eliminating the generation cost of evidence, and allows direct measurement of the evidence tax at each model scale. Detailed hyperparameters (learning rate, batch size, number of epochs) are reported in the Appendix.
16	
17	
18	\subsection{Optimization Paradigms}
19	\label{sec:paradigms}
20	
21	We evaluate three post-SFT optimization paradigms that differ in how they incorporate quality signals from the CED scoring function (\S\ref{sec:ced}).
22	
23	\paragraph{GRPO.}
24	Group Relative Policy Optimization~\citep{shao2024deepseekmath} is an online RL method that eliminates the need for a separate value network by computing advantages within groups of sampled outputs. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We test two configurations: QLoRA \citep{dettmers2023qlora} under 4-bit quantization, and bf16 full-precision LoRA, both with $G{=}8$ groups. Because GRPO applies gradient updates that directly optimize the reward signal, evidence quality and extraction accuracy compete for gradient capacity. We analyze this multi-objective tension in Section~\ref{sec:spectrum}.
25	
26	\paragraph{DPO.}
27	Direct Preference Optimization~\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF~\citep{ouyang2022instructgpt} as a classification problem over preference pairs, without explicit reward models. We construct preference data from the SFT model's sampled outputs: for each document, the response with the highest CED score is labeled as preferred and the lowest-scoring response as rejected. DPO then adjusts the policy to increase the log-probability ratio between preferred and rejected completions. We train for 1 and 3 epochs to observe the temporal dynamics of evidence-extraction trade-offs under implicit optimization.
28	
29	\paragraph{RSFT.}
30	Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning is a single-round variant of iterative self-improvement methods such as STaR~\citep{zelikman2022star}. The procedure is straightforward: for each SFT checkpoint, the corresponding model generates $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per training document, each candidate is scored by CED, and only the top-$k{=}1$ response is retained as training data. In our primary experiments (4B, 8B, LLaMA-8B), all RSFT seeds share the same SFT s42 checkpoint and the same CED-scored training data; only the LoRA initialization and data shuffling seeds differ during the final fine-tuning stage. At 14B, seed-matched SFT checkpoints are used. We use $k{=}1$ (best-of-$N$) selection throughout all experiments, selecting the single highest-scoring candidate per document. This yields approximately 3,000 training examples per model (one per document; exact counts vary slightly by model due to generation failures). Unlike GRPO and DPO, RSFT does not apply gradient-based optimization pressure toward a reward signal. Instead, it improves training data quality through filtering: the model trains on its own best outputs rather than all outputs. This distinction is critical because discrete selection over complete outputs cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find. The contribution of RSFT in our setting is not the framework itself, which is well-established, but its application to evidence-grounded DocRE with CED as a domain-specific quality filter.
31	
32	
33	\subsection{Contrastive Evidence Discrimination}
34	\label{sec:ced}
35	
36	CED is a scoring function that evaluates the faithfulness of predicted evidence sentences to their associated relation triples. It builds on Natural Language Inference (NLI) to measure whether evidence actually entails the predicted relation, rather than merely co-occurring with the relevant entities.
37	
38	For a predicted triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$ with evidence sentences $E$, CED constructs a positive NLI pair: the premise is the concatenation of sentences in $E$, and the hypothesis is a natural-language verbalization of the relation (e.g., ``$e_h$ has relation $r$ with $e_t$''). An NLI model based on DeBERTaV3 \citep{he2023debertav3} (\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}) produces an entailment probability $p^+$ for this pair. The contrastive component introduces hard negatives drawn from the same document. For each triple, we collect sentences that mention $e_h$ or $e_t$ but are not in the predicted evidence set. These entity-sharing distractors serve as negative premises, and the NLI model scores each to obtain $p^-_1, \ldots, p^-_m$. The CED score for a single triple is:
39	\begin{equation}
40	\text{CED}(e_h, r, e_t, E) = p^+ - \max_j \, p^-_j
41	\label{eq:ced}
42	\end{equation}
43	\begin{figure}[t]
44	\centering
45	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_ced_scoring.pdf}
46	\caption{CED scoring example. The positive pair (evidence $\to$ hypothesis) yields high entailment; the contrastive distractor (entity-sharing non-evidence) yields low entailment. CED measures the margin between them.}
47	\label{fig:ced_scoring}
48	\end{figure}
49	This formulation forces the scoring function to distinguish sentences that genuinely support the relation from sentences that merely mention the same entities. A high CED score indicates that the evidence provides relation-specific support beyond entity co-occurrence.
50	
51	The document-level CED score aggregates over all predicted triples, weighted by extraction correctness: triples that match ground-truth relations contribute their CED scores, while incorrect triples receive zero credit. This coupling ensures that CED rewards both faithful evidence and accurate extraction jointly.
52	
53	We also evaluate a \emph{flat-NLI} baseline that uses only the positive entailment score $p^+$ without the contrastive term. CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (mean Rel-F1 difference of 0.02pp across available seeds; Section~\ref{sec:analysis}), confirming that the contrastive formulation is not essential for data selection quality. We adopt CED as the default because its per-triple discrimination scores support interpretable error analysis.
54	
55	
56	\subsection{Evidence-Decoupling Ratio}
57	\label{sec:edcr}
58	
59	For analysis, we define the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric that quantifies the degree of misalignment between a model's extraction and evidence capabilities. EDCR is not a training component; it serves purely as an analytical tool for comparing optimization methods.
60	
61	EDCR is defined as the distractor citation rate across all predicted triples:
62	\begin{equation}
63	\text{EDCR} = \frac{\sum_{i} |E_i^{\text{pred}} \setminus E_i^{\text{gold}}|}{\sum_{i} |E_i^{\text{pred}}|}
64	\label{eq:edcr}
65	\end{equation}
66	where the sum ranges over all predicted triples with at least one evidence citation. $E_i^{\text{pred}}$ is the set of evidence sentences cited for predicted triple $i$, and $E_i^{\text{gold}}$ is the corresponding gold evidence (empty for false-positive predictions). EDCR thus measures the fraction of all cited evidence that does not appear in the gold evidence for the associated triple.
67	
68	A model with many false-positive triples will have high EDCR because all evidence for incorrect triples counts as distractor; even among correct triples, citing irrelevant sentences inflates the numerator. Conversely, accurate extraction with faithful evidence yields low EDCR. This dual sensitivity makes EDCR a natural diagnostic for extraction-evidence decoupling.
69	
70	EDCR operates at two diagnostic levels. At the method level, comparing EDCR across seven unique method configurations reveals a negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.96$, $n{=}7$, $p{<}0.001$). Because EDCR is mechanically influenced by false-positive triples (which share determinants with Rel-F1), this correlation should be interpreted as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent discovery; its primary value lies in per-relation analysis. We validate EDCR's diagnostic utility through per-relation analysis (\S\ref{sec:analysis}), which avoids the shared-component concern present in cross-method correlation. At the per-relation level, computing EDCR for individual relation types reveals a robust negative correlation with Rel-F1 ($\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$) that is independent of Evi-F1 ($\rho{=}{-}0.06$, $p{=}0.58$). This independence means EDCR captures relation-specific extraction-evidence misalignment that aggregate evidence metrics miss. The SFT baseline EDCR (0.6797, three-seed mean) reflects the inherent distractor rate under standard training; EDCR values substantially exceeding this level (e.g., GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876) indicate RL-induced decoupling beyond what evidence production alone explains, while values below the baseline (e.g., RSFT at 0.6487) reflect improved co-optimization.
71	
```

This dual sensitivity makes EDCR a natural diagnostic for extraction-evidence decoupling.\n\nEDCR operates at two diagnostic levels. At the method level, comparing EDCR across seven unique method configurations reveals a negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.96$, $n{=}7$, $p{<}0.001$). Because EDCR is mechanically influenced by false-positive triples (which share determinants with Rel-F1), this correlation should be interpreted as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent discovery; its primary value lies in per-relation analysis. We validate EDCR's diagnostic utility through per-relation analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}), which avoids the shared-component concern present in cross-method correlation. At the per-relation level, computing EDCR for individual relation types reveals a robust negative correlation with Rel-F1 ($\\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$) that is independent of Evi-F1 ($\\rho{=}{-}0.06$, $p{=}0.58$). This independence means EDCR captures relation-specific extraction-evidence misalignment that aggregate evidence metrics miss. The SFT baseline EDCR (0.6797, three-seed mean) reflects the inherent distractor rate under standard training; EDCR values substantially exceeding this level (e.g., GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876) indicate RL-induced decoupling beyond what evidence production alone explains, while values below the baseline (e.g., RSFT at 0.6487) reflect improved co-optimization.\n",

    }

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We evaluate on DocRED~\citep{yao2019docred} (3,053 training / 998 dev documents, 96 relation types) with Re-DocRED~\citep{tan2022redocred} for cross-dataset validation. Our primary model is Qwen3-4B; we also train Qwen3-1.7B, 8B, and 14B to study scale effects, with LLaMA-3.1-8B for cross-family validation. All models use LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} ($r{=}64$, $\alpha{=}128$) with structured output jointly producing triples and evidence. RSFT selects the top-$k{=}1$ of $N{=}8$ samples by CED score (\S\ref{sec:ced}); GRPO uses $G{=}8$ groups under both QLoRA and bf16; DPO constructs preference pairs from CED-ranked outputs. We report Ign-F1 (primary; ignores training-set triples), Rel-F1, Evi-F1 (TP-only and joint per DREEAM protocol~\citep{ma2023dreeam}), and EDCR (\S\ref{sec:edcr}). Detailed hyperparameters appear in the Appendix.
8	
9	
10	\subsection{Main Results}
11	\label{sec:main_results}
12	
13	Table~\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison on Qwen3-4B. The SFT baseline exhibits high seed sensitivity (mean Ign-F1 43.58, std 3.80pp, range 39.74--47.34). Removing evidence entirely (no-evidence SFT, s42) reaches 47.21 Ign-F1.
14	
15	RSFT CED achieves 44.82 Ign-F1 (+1.24 over SFT mean) with substantially lower variance (std 0.51pp), though these reflect different sources of variation (Section~\ref{sec:scaling}). Even the weakest RSFT seed (44.23) exceeds the SFT mean. Evidence quality improves (Evi-F1 80.96 vs.\ 79.61) and coupling tightens (EDCR 0.6487 vs.\ 0.6797). Per-seed results appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:ablation_full}.
16	
17	\begin{table}[t]
18	\centering
19	\caption{Main results on DocRED dev (Qwen3-4B). Ign-F1 shows mean $\pm$ std$^{(\text{seeds})}$; other metrics are same-seed means. Best \textbf{bold}; best evidence-generating \underline{underlined}. Per-seed: Appendix~\ref{tab:ablation_full}.}
20	\label{tab:main}
21	\small
22	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
23	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
24	\toprule
25	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Ign-F1} & \textbf{Rel-F1} & \textbf{Evi-F1} & \textbf{EDCR} \\
26	\midrule
27	SFT baseline & 43.58{\scriptsize$\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 44.39 & 79.61 & 0.6797 \\
28	SFT no-evidence & \textbf{47.21} & \textbf{48.76} & -- & -- \\
29	RSFT CED & \underline{44.82{\scriptsize$\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$} & \underline{45.80} & 80.96 & 0.6487 \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}%
32	}
33	\vspace{-2mm}
34	\end{table}
35	
36	\begin{figure}[t]
37	\centering
38	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_scaling.pdf}
39	\caption{Evidence tax and RSFT gain across model scales. The gap between no-evidence and SFT bars reveals the scale-dependent evidence tax; RSFT consistently narrows this gap.}
40	\label{fig:scaling}
41	\end{figure}
42	
43	
44	\subsection{Evidence-Extraction Decoupling Spectrum}
45	\label{sec:spectrum}
46	
47	Because evidence and extraction share gradient capacity, different optimization paradigms handle this competition in qualitatively different ways. We organize the outcomes along a \emph{decoupling spectrum} from catastrophic collapse to no decoupling (Figure~\ref{fig:spectrum}; full data in Table~\ref{tab:spectrum}, Appendix). Figure~\ref{fig:grpo_dynamics} traces these dynamics across training checkpoints.
48	
49	\begin{figure*}[!t]
50	\centering
51	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig_grpo_dynamics.pdf}
52	\caption{GRPO training dynamics (both $G{=}8$). QLoRA (4-bit) suffers catastrophic extraction collapse at step~10 while evidence remains intact; bf16 (full-precision) maintains stable Rel-F1 throughout. EDCR confirms near-total decoupling under quantization-constrained precision.}
53	\label{fig:grpo_dynamics}
54	\end{figure*}
55	
56	\paragraph{GRPO.}
57	Both QLoRA and bf16 configurations use $G{=}8$ groups with identical learning rate ($5{\times}10^{-5}$) and KL coefficient (0.01). Under QLoRA 4-bit quantization, GRPO drives Rel-F1 to 1.38 while Evi-F1 rises to 85.04 (EDCR 0.9876): quantization-constrained precision limits the effective parameter space, so the reward signal preferentially optimizes the easier sub-task~\citep{gao2023scaling}, resembling the quantized-actor divergence of \citet{li2026qurl}. Full-precision bf16 avoids catastrophe (Rel-F1 45.49, EDCR 0.6318). Because $G$, learning rate, and KL coefficient are shared, the only configuration differences are quantization precision (4-bit vs.\ bf16) and effective batch size (16 vs.\ 1 via gradient accumulation). The larger effective batch in QLoRA typically promotes training stability, yet QLoRA collapses catastrophically while bf16 does not, strongly implicating 4-bit quantization precision loss as the dominant factor (a supplementary bf16 $G{=}4$ run further confirms group size is not a factor; Appendix~\ref{sec:grpo_dynamics}).
58	
59	\paragraph{DPO and RSFT.}
60	DPO exhibits substantial extraction degradation: Rel-F1 drops from 33.37 (1 epoch) to 31.45 (3 epochs) while Evi-F1 rises (80.90$\to$84.84). EDCR is non-monotonic (0.8123$\to$0.7503): despite worsening extraction, evidence selectivity improves with continued training, partially reducing the decoupling. RSFT maintains both capabilities simultaneously (Rel-F1 45.80, Evi-F1 80.96, EDCR 0.6487) because discrete output selection cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off.
61	
62	\begin{figure}[t]
63	\centering
64	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_spectrum.pdf}
65	\caption{Decoupling spectrum. Each method is plotted by Rel-F1 (x-axis) and Evi-F1 (y-axis). Arrows indicate training progression for DPO and GRPO bf16. Decoupled methods drift toward the upper-left.}
66	\label{fig:spectrum}
67	\end{figure}
68	
69	
70	\subsection{Scale-Dependent Evidence Tax}
71	\label{sec:scaling}
72	
73	The evidence tax (Ign-F1(no-evidence) minus Ign-F1(with-evidence), so positive values indicate cost and negative values indicate benefit) is scale-dependent. Under matched evaluation (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$2048, format-compliant intersection; Appendix~\ref{sec:training_tax}), the training-time tax reveals a within-family capacity threshold: evidence generation benefits 1.7B ($-$5.63pp), where it also halves the output duplication rate (60.5\%$\to$33.0\%; Table~\ref{tab:mechanistic} in Appendix~\ref{sec:fair_subset}), but is approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B, $n{=}3$; +0.04pp at 8B; +0.03pp at 14B). The 1.7B and 8B no-evidence baselines use a single seed (s42); the 1.7B s42 is the strongest SFT seed (+3.22pp above the three-seed mean), so the 1.7B benefit may be amplified relative to the population mean. Table~\ref{tab:scaling} and Figure~\ref{fig:evidence_tax} present RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across five models (Figure~\ref{fig:scaling}).
74	
75	RSFT navigates this capacity landscape through quality-based data selection rather than gradient pressure. Mean Ign-F1 improves across tested scales: +6.55pp at 1.7B, +1.24pp at 4B, +4.14pp at 8B, and +3.44pp at 14B (Table~\ref{tab:scaling}).
76	
77	We observe lower cross-seed variance under RSFT at all four tested configurations (SFT std $>$ RSFT std; Appendix~\ref{sec:variance_ci}). However, at 4B, 8B, and LLaMA-8B, RSFT seeds share both the SFT checkpoint and CED-scored training data from seed 42, differing only in fine-tuning randomness. The observed low RSFT variance therefore largely reflects shared initialization and training data rather than an intrinsic property of the RSFT methodology. Only at 1.7B do the $\chi^2$ confidence intervals not overlap, but this comparison is subject to the same shared-base limitation. Separately, the 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 mean Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19) at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters.
78	
79	\begin{table}[t]
80	\centering
81	\caption{RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across model scales (DocRED dev Ign-F1). LLaMA-3.1-8B included for cross-family validation. Mean $\pm$ std$^{(\text{seeds})}$.}
82	\label{tab:scaling}
83	\small
84	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
85	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
86	\toprule
87	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{SFT w/ evi} & \textbf{RSFT} & \textbf{RSFT Tax}$^\dagger$ & \textbf{RSFT Gain} \\
88	\midrule
89	Qwen3-1.7B & 44.64{\scriptsize$\pm$2.79}$^{(3)}$ & 51.19{\scriptsize$\pm$0.09}$^{(3)}$ & $-$7.75 & +6.55 \\
90	Qwen3-4B & 43.58{\scriptsize$\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 44.82{\scriptsize$\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$ & +0.94 & +1.24 \\
91	Qwen3-8B & 50.19{\scriptsize$\pm$3.23}$^{(3)}$ & 54.33{\scriptsize$\pm$0.50}$^{(3)}$ & $-$0.78 & +4.14 \\
92	Qwen3-14B & 55.07$^{(1)}$ & 58.51{\scriptsize$\pm$0.50}$^{(2)}$ & $-$2.78{\scriptsize$\pm$2.49}$^{(2)}$ & +3.44 \\
93	LLaMA-3.1-8B & 51.09{\scriptsize$\pm$2.92}$^{(3)}$ & 56.54{\scriptsize$\pm$0.64}$^{(3)}$ & $-$2.17{\scriptsize$\pm$0.76}$^{(3)}$ & +5.45 \\
94	\bottomrule
95	\end{tabular}%
96	}
97	\vspace{1mm}
98	{\raggedright\footnotesize $^\dagger$Inference-time: same RSFT model with/without evidence. No-evi uses @2048; with-evi uses @1024 (1.7B: @2048). Tax $=$ no-evi $-$ with-evi; positive $=$ cost. Training-time tax in Appendix~\ref{sec:training_tax}.\par}
99	\end{table}
100	
101	\begin{figure}[t]
102	\centering
103	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_evidence_tax.pdf}
104	\vspace{-2mm}
105	\caption{Inference-time evidence tax across model scales (RSFT models, Re-DocRED test). Positive $=$ cost, negative $=$ benefit. Error bars: $\pm$1 std (all scales $\geq$2 seeds).}
106	\label{fig:evidence_tax}
107	\end{figure}
108	
109	\subsection{Evidence Strategy Comparison}
110	\label{sec:evidence_strategy}
111	
112	We compare joint generation against post-hoc BM25 retrieval (entity-constrained, $k{=}2$, matching the median gold evidence count per triple in DocRED), using Evi-F1 (joint) with the DREEAM-protocol denominator~\citep{ma2023dreeam}. Post-hoc retrieval achieves higher Rel-F1 (48.76) by avoiding the evidence tax but lower Evi-F1 (joint) of 34.7 (EDCR 0.766). RSFT CED reaches 36.1 (EDCR 0.6487), a 1.4-point evidence advantage. CED reranking at inference (best-of-$N{=}8$ by CED score) achieves Rel-F1 48.64 and Evi-F1 81.88 (TP-only), approaching no-evidence extraction quality while preserving evidence.
113	
114	
115	\subsection{Generalization}
116	\label{sec:generalization}
117	
118	Cross-architecture validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (Table~\ref{tab:scaling}) shows the largest RSFT gain (+5.45pp) with complete pairwise seed separation (\S\ref{sec:scaling}). The RSFT improvement transfers across datasets: on Re-DocRED~\citep{tan2022redocred}, RSFT achieves 41.45 Ign-F1 (+4.86pp over SFT 36.59), matching the DocRED gain (+5.08pp). Our best joint Evi-F1 (36.1, DREEAM-protocol) falls below supervised DREEAM (54.15\%~\citep{ma2023dreeam}), a gap driven by extraction performance rather than evidence quality (TP-only Evi-F1 exceeds 80\%). On the Re-DocRED test set, RSFT Ign-F1 drops approximately 20pp relative to dev across all evaluated scales (e.g., 14B: 58.5$\to$38.5), attributable to 2.82$\times$ higher annotation density in test (34.7 vs.\ 12.3 gold triples per document); model ranking is perfectly preserved (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$; Table~\ref{tab:test_results} in Appendix). Full results appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:generalization_full}.
119	
120	
121	\subsection{Discussion}
122	\label{sec:analysis}
123	
124	EDCR validates at both cross-method ($\rho = -0.96$, $p < 0.001$) and per-relation ($\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$, $p < 0.001$) levels, the latter orthogonal to Evi-F1 ($\rho = -0.06$); partial correlations are virtually unchanged when controlling for shared components (Appendix~\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes; stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:ablation_full}). RSFT reduces truncation, hallucination, and duplication errors by 6--22\% (Figure~\ref{fig:error_decomposition}) but does not improve evidence quality ($\Delta < 0.4$pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:error_analysis}): it improves \emph{what} the model extracts, not \emph{how well} it grounds extractions.
125	
126	\begin{figure}[t]
127	\centering
128	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_error_decomposition.pdf}
129	\caption{Relative change in error categories from SFT to RSFT across scales. Negative values indicate improvement; the residual FN increase reflects a shift toward harder remaining errors.}
130	\label{fig:error_decomposition}
131	\end{figure}
132

However, at 4B, 8B, and LLaMA-8B, RSFT seeds share both the SFT checkpoint and CED-scored training data from seed 42, differing only in fine-tuning randomness. The observed low RSFT variance therefore largely reflects shared initialization and training data rather than an intrinsic property of the RSFT methodology. Only at 1.7B do the $\\chi^2$ confidence intervals not overlap, but this comparison is subject to the same shared-base limitation. Separately, the 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 mean Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across model scales (DocRED dev Ign-F1). LLaMA-3.1-8B included for cross-family validation. Mean $\\pm$ std$^{(\\text{seeds})}$.}\n\\label{tab:scaling}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{SFT w/ evi} & \\textbf{RSFT} & \\textbf{RSFT Tax}$^\\dagger$ & \\textbf{RSFT Gain} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-1.7B & 44.64{\\scriptsize$\\pm$2.79}$^{(3)}$ & 51.19{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.09}$^{(3)}$ & $-$7.75 & +6.55 \\\\\nQwen3-4B & 43.58{\\scriptsize$\\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 44.82{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$ & +0.94 & +1.24 \\\\\nQwen3-8B & 50.19{\\scriptsize$\\pm$3.23}$^{(3)}$ & 54.33{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.50}$^{(3)}$ & $-$0.78 & +4.14 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & 55.07$^{(1)}$ & 58.51{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.50}$^{(2)}$ & $-$2.78{\\scriptsize$\\pm$2.49}$^{(2)}$ & +3.44 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & 51.09{\\scriptsize$\\pm$2.92}$^{(3)}$ & 56.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.64}$^{(3)}$ & $-$2.17{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.76}$^{(3)}$ & +5.45 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{1mm}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Inference-time: same RSFT model with/without evidence. No-evi uses @2048; with-evi uses @1024 (1.7B: @2048). Tax $=$ no-evi $-$ with-evi; positive $=$ cost. Training-time tax in Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_tax}.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_evidence_tax.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Inference-time evidence tax across model scales (RSFT models, Re-DocRED test). Positive $=$ cost, negative $=$ benefit. Error bars: $\\pm$1 std (all scales $\\geq$2 seeds).}\n\\label{fig:evidence_tax}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Evidence Strategy Comparison}\n\\label{sec:evidence_strategy}\n\nWe compare joint generation against post-hoc BM25 retrieval (entity-constrained, $k{=}2$, matching the median gold evidence count per triple in DocRED), using Evi-F1 (joint) with the DREEAM-protocol denominator~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}. Post-hoc retrieval achieves higher Rel-F1 (48.76) by avoiding the evidence tax but lower Evi-F1 (joint) of 34.7 (EDCR 0.766). RSFT CED reaches 36.1 (EDCR 0.6487), a 1.4-point evidence advantage. CED reranking at inference (best-of-$N{=}8$ by CED score) achieves Rel-F1 48.64 and Evi-F1 81.88 (TP-only), approaching no-evidence extraction quality while preserving evidence.\n\n\n\\subsection{Generalization}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nCross-architecture validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (Table~\\ref{tab:scaling}) shows the largest RSFT gain (+5.45pp) with complete pairwise seed separation (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). The RSFT improvement transfers across datasets: on Re-DocRED~\\citep{tan2022redocred}, RSFT achieves 41.45 Ign-F1 (+4.86pp over SFT 36.59), matching the DocRED gain (+5.08pp). Our best joint Evi-F1 (36.1, DREEAM-protocol) falls below supervised DREEAM (54.15\\%~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}), a gap driven by extraction performance rather than evidence quality (TP-only Evi-F1 exceeds 80\\%). On the Re-DocRED test set, RSFT Ign-F1 drops approximately 20pp relative to dev across all evaluated scales (e.g., 14B: 58.5$\\to$38.5), attributable to 2.82$\\times$ higher annotation density in test (34.7 vs.\\ 12.3 gold triples per document); model ranking is perfectly preserved (Spearman $\\rho{=}1.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:test_results} in Appendix). Full results appear in Appendix~\\ref{sec:generalization_full}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nEDCR validates at both cross-method ($\\rho = -0.96$, $p < 0.001$) and per-relation ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$, $p < 0.001$) levels, the latter orthogonal to Evi-F1 ($\\rho = -0.06$); partial correlations are virtually unchanged when controlling for shared components (Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes; stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}). RSFT reduces truncation, hallucination, and duplication errors by 6--22\\% (Figure~\\ref{fig:error_decomposition}) but does not improve evidence quality ($\\Delta < 0.4$pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_analysis}): it improves \\emph{what} the model extracts, not \\emph{how well} it grounds extractions.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_error_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Relative change in error categories from SFT to RSFT across scales. Negative values indicate improvement; the residual FN increase reflects a shift toward harder remaining errors.}\n\\label{fig:error_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n",

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\subsection{Document-Level Relation Extraction}
5	\label{sec:rw_docre}
6	
7	Encoder-based DocRE methods treat relation identification as classification over entity pair representations, with ATLOP \citep{zhou2021atlop} introducing adaptive thresholding and graph-based methods such as GAIN \citep{zeng2020gain} modeling inter-sentence reasoning. A parallel thread adds sentence-level evidence: SAIS \citep{xiao2022sais} introduced evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, DREEAM \citep{ma2023dreeam} extended this with dynamic memory-based retrieval, and EIDER \citep{xie2022eider} fuses evidence into the relation classifier. In all three, evidence is binary sentence classification that regularizes the encoder without increasing output length.
8	
9	Recent LLM-based methods recast DocRE as generation: AutoRE \citep{autore2024} uses QLoRA with relation-head-facts decomposition, \citet{xu2024lmrc} frame extraction as reading comprehension, RelPrior \citep{wang2025relprior} leverages relation priors, and \citet{xu2025docksrag} combine retrieval with structured knowledge. These methods match or surpass encoder baselines but produce bare triplets without evidence. At 8B, our RSFT model (54.33 Ign-F1) outperforms AutoRE (52.14\footnote{AutoRE Ign-F1 from the LLaMA3-8B reproduction by \citet{wang2025relprior}; the original paper reports only F1.}) and is slightly below RelPrior ($-$1.17pp), while being the only method providing sentence-level evidence. The critical distinction is computational: in encoder methods, evidence retrieval is classification at negligible cost; in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires generating additional tokens, imposing costs that depend on model capacity and optimization strategy.
10	
11	\subsection{Reinforcement Learning for Structured Generation}
12	\label{sec:rw_rl}
13	
14	GRPO \citep{shao2024deepseekmath} replaces PPO's \citep{schulman2017ppo} value network with group-relative advantages; DeepSeek-R1 \citep{deepseek2025r1} demonstrates its effectiveness at scale. Subsequent work addresses failure modes such as entropy collapse \citep{yu2025dapo} and length bias \citep{liu2025drgrpo}; applications to structured IE include R1-RE \citep{dai2025r1re} and REAR \citep{wang2025rear}. DPO \citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF \citep{ouyang2022instructgpt} as implicit reward maximization. Our decoupling spectrum differs from catastrophic forgetting in RL alignment \citep{jin2025rl_panacea}: extraction and evidence compete \emph{within} a single task, resembling negative transfer in multi-task learning \citep{ruder2017overview}, amplified by QLoRA's \citep{dettmers2023qlora} constrained parameter space and capacity-dependent scaling dynamics \citep{gao2023scaling}.
15	
16	\subsection{Evidence Faithfulness and Rejection Sampling}
17	\label{sec:rw_faithfulness}
18	
19	NLI-based faithfulness evaluation \citep{laban2022summac,zhang2024infuse} and reward-based evidence grounding \citep{li2025srvf,tamo2026evidencerl} have addressed rationale quality in generation tasks. Our CED scoring targets evidence-relation entailment rather than free-form rationales. STaR \citep{zelikman2022star} introduced iterative self-improvement through rejection sampling; subsequent scaling analyses \citep{gulcehre2023rest,yuan2023rft} and theoretical connections to PPO \citep{tang2025rsftppo} have established the foundations of sample-and-select methods. RSFT extends this principle to structured IE, preserving extraction-evidence coupling through discrete selection rather than gradient pressure.
20	
```

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We study evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction as a structured generation task. The evidence tax is scale-dependent and shaped by optimization paradigm: gradient methods amplify extraction-evidence competition while RSFT avoids it through discrete selection. RSFT improves mean extraction across all tested scales; a 1.7B RSFT model approaches 8B SFT performance at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters. Future work should investigate targeted evidence rewards and extend evaluation beyond Wikipedia.
5

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RuaJgBTZbWvVLxqSt5Swmv`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large Language Model (LLM)-based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) now matches or exceeds encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet outputs bare relation triplets without identifying supporting sentences. Encoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) provide evidence but trail LLM approaches in accuracy, creating a gap between extraction performance and verifiability. We study evidence-grounded DocRE as structured generation and compare Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT) across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. The evidence tax (extraction cost of generating evidence) is scale-dependent: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp dev Ign-F1) but approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B; positive $=$ cost). Gradient-based optimization amplifies evidence-extraction competition (GRPO catastrophic, DPO gradual), while RSFT with NLI-based scoring avoids decoupling through output filtering rather than gradient pressure. We define the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) to quantify extraction-evidence misalignment, validated at per-relation level ($\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1) approaches 8B Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) performance (50.19) at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters. Test-set evaluation preserves model ranking across all scales (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$). We release our code and data at\footnote{\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/faith-evidence-ie}}.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JvjykoWAGSmpgoM9HXV79r`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Data and architecture scope.}
4	We evaluate on two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), both derived from Wikipedia; cross-domain evaluation is constrained by the absence of evidence-annotated DocRE datasets beyond Wikipedia, a limitation shared by all prior evidence-aware work (SAIS, DREEAM, EIDER).
5	Evidence tax patterns span two model families (Qwen3, LLaMA) with single hyperparameter settings per RL method; other architectures, scales beyond 14B, or alternative KL coefficients may produce different decoupling behaviors. The decoupling spectrum analysis is conducted entirely at 4B scale; whether the same ordering (GRPO catastrophic $\to$ DPO gradual $\to$ RSFT none) holds at other scales remains unverified.
6	
7	\paragraph{CED scoring sensitivity.}
8	CED scoring operates at the document level using an external NLI model (DeBERTa-v3); sensitivity to other NLI model families is unexplored, and sentence-level CED filtering disrupts co-reference chains (Evi-F1 $-$7.7pp).
9	
10	\paragraph{RSFT mechanistic explanation.}
11	Our explanation for why RSFT avoids decoupling (that discrete selection cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off) is structural rather than empirical. We have not isolated whether RSFT's advantage stems primarily from removing low-quality training examples, from implicit data augmentation through candidate diversity, or from both. Disentangling these factors would require controlled experiments with fixed training data quality but varied selection mechanisms.
12	
```

</details>

We report Ign-F1 (primary; ignores training-set triples), Rel-F1, Evi-F1 (TP-only and joint per DREEAM protocol~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}), and EDCR (\\S\\ref{sec:edcr}). ```text
70	\subsection{Scale-Dependent Evidence Tax}
71	\label{sec:scaling}
72	
73	The evidence tax (Ign-F1(no-evidence) minus Ign-F1(with-evidence), so positive values indicate cost and negative values indicate benefit) is scale-dependent. Under matched evaluation (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$2048, format-compliant intersection; Appendix~\ref{sec:training_tax}), the training-time tax reveals a within-family capacity threshold: evidence generation benefits 1.7B ($-$5.63pp), where it also halves the output duplication rate (60.5\%$\to$33.0\%; Table~\ref{tab:mechanistic} in Appendix~\ref{sec:fair_subset}), but is approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B, $n{=}3$; +0.04pp at 8B; +0.03pp at 14B). The 1.7B and 8B no-evidence baselines use a single seed (s42); the 1.7B s42 is the strongest SFT seed (+3.22pp above the three-seed mean), so the 1.7B benefit may be amplified relative to the population mean. Table~\ref{tab:scaling} and Figure~\ref{fig:evidence_tax} present RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across five models (Figure~\ref{fig:scaling}).
74	
75	RSFT navigates this capacity landscape through quality-based data selection rather than gradient pressure. Mean Ign-F1 improves across tested scales: +6.55pp at 1.7B, +1.24pp at 4B, +4.14pp at 8B, and +3.44pp at 14B (Table~\ref{tab:scaling}).
76	
77	We observe lower cross-seed variance under RSFT at all four tested configurations (SFT std $>$ RSFT std; Appendix~\ref{sec:variance_ci}). However, at 4B, 8B, and LLaMA-8B, RSFT seeds share both the SFT checkpoint and CED-scored training data from seed 42, differing only in fine-tuning randomness. The observed low RSFT variance therefore largely reflects shared initialization and training data rather than an intrinsic property of the RSFT methodology. Only at 1.7B do the $\chi^2$ confidence intervals not overlap, but this comparison is subject to the same shared-base limitation. Separately, the 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 mean Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19) at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters.
78	
79	\begin{table}[t]
80	\centering
81	\caption{RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across model scales (DocRED dev Ign-F1). LLaMA-3.1-8B included for cross-family validation. Mean $\pm$ std$^{(\text{seeds})}$.}
82	\label{tab:scaling}
83	\small
84	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
85	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
86	\toprule
87	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{SFT w/ evi} & \textbf{RSFT} & \textbf{RSFT Tax}$^\dagger$ & \textbf{RSFT Gain} \\
88	\midrule
89	Qwen3-1.7B & 44.64{\scriptsize$\pm$2.79}$^{(3)}$ & 51.19{\scriptsize$\pm$0.09}$^{(3)}$ & $-$7.75 & +6.55 \\
90	Qwen3-4B & 43.58{\scriptsize$\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 44.82{\scriptsize$\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$ & +0.94 & +1.24 \\
91	Qwen3-8B & 50.19{\scriptsize$\pm$3.23}$^{(3)}$ & 54.33{\scriptsize$\pm$0.50}$^{(3)}$ & $-$0.78 & +4.14 \\
92	Qwen3-14B & 55.07$^{(1)}$ & 58.51{\scriptsize$\pm$0.50}$^{(2)}$ & $-$2.78{\scriptsize$\pm$2.49}$^{(2)}$ & +3.44 \\
93	LLaMA-3.1-8B & 51.09{\scriptsize$\pm$2.92}$^{(3)}$ & 56.54{\scriptsize$\pm$0.64}$^{(3)}$ & $-$2.17{\scriptsize$\pm$0.76}$^{(3)}$ & +5.45 \\
94	\bottomrule
95	\end{tabular}%
96	}
97	\vspace{1mm}
98	{\raggedright\footnotesize $^\dagger$Inference-time: same RSFT model with/without evidence. No-evi uses @2048; with-evi uses @1024 (1.7B: @2048). Tax $=$ no-evi $-$ with-evi; positive $=$ cost. Training-time tax in Appendix~\ref{sec:training_tax}.\par}
99	\end{table}

          " ",
          " The evidence tax (Ign-F1(no-evidence) minus Ign-F1(with-evidence), so positive values indicate cost and negative values indicate benefit) is scale-dependent. Under matched evaluation (\\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}$=$2048, format-compliant intersection; Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_tax}), the training-time tax reveals a within-family capacity threshold: evidence generation benefits 1.7B ($-$5.63pp), where it also halves the output duplication rate (60.5\\%$\\to$33.0\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:mechanistic} in Appendix~\\ref{sec:fair_subset}), but is approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B, $n{=}3$; +0.04pp at 8B; +0.03pp at 14B). The 1.7B and 8B no-evidence baselines use a single seed (s42); the 1.7B s42 is the strongest SFT seed (+3.22pp above the three-seed mean), so the 1.7B benefit may be amplified relative to the population mean. Table~\\ref{tab:scaling} and Figure~\\ref{fig:evidence_tax} present RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across five models (Figure~\\ref{fig:scaling}).",
          " ",
          "-RSFT navigates this capacity landscape through quality-based data selection rather than gradient pressure. Mean Ign-F1 improves across tested scales: +6.55pp at 1.7B, +1.24pp at 4B, +4.14pp at 8B, and +3.44pp at 14B (Table~\\ref{tab:scaling}).",
          "+RSFT navigates this capacity landscape through quality-based data selection rather than gradient pressure. Mean Ign-F1 improves across tested scales: +6.55pp at 1.7B, +1.24pp at 4B, +4.14pp at 8B, and +3.44pp at 14B (Table~\\ref{tab:scaling}; per-scale Rel-F1 and Evi-F1 breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:scaling_details}).",
          " ",
          " We observe lower cross-seed variance under RSFT at all four tested configurations (SFT std $>$ RSFT std; Appendix~\\ref{sec:variance_ci}). However, at 4B, 8B, and LLaMA-8B, RSFT seeds share both the SFT checkpoint and CED-scored training data from seed 42, differing only in fine-tuning randomness. The observed low RSFT variance therefore largely reflects shared initialization and training data rather than an intrinsic property of the RSFT methodology. Only at 1.7B do the $\\chi^2$ confidence intervals not overlap, but this comparison is subject to the same shared-base limitation. Separately, the 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 mean Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters.",
          " "
        ]

    "newString": "RSFT improves \\emph{what} the model extracts but not \\emph{how well} it grounds extractions. Appendix~\\ref{sec:qualitative} presents representative examples illustrating evidence precision differences, GRPO format gaming under collapse, and per-relation EDCR diagnostics.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{EDCR as a diagnostic.}\nEDCR (\\S\\ref{sec:edcr}) correlates with extraction quality at two levels: cross-method ($\\rho = -0.89$, $n = 7$, $p = 0.007$; Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum}; $\\rho = -0.83$, $n = 6$, $p = 0.042$ excluding the GRPO QLoRA outlier) and per-relation ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$, $p < 0.001$), the latter providing validation independent of method-level sample size and orthogonal to Evi-F1 ($\\rho = -0.06$, $p = 0.58$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). EDCR shares TP in both numerator and denominator with Rel-F1, contributing to the observed negative correlation; partial correlations controlling for Evi-Recall$_\\text{TP}$ are virtually unchanged ($\\rho = -0.8541$ vs.\\ $-0.8554$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). RSFT avoids decoupling because discrete output selection cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off (Appendix~\\ref{sec:rsft_theory}). CED operates effectively at the response level but fails at sentence-level granularity (Evi-F1 $-$7.7pp), as filtering individual sentences disrupts co-reference chains.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation.}\nCED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT performance (Rel-F1 45.63 vs.\\ 45.61); stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ by 1.02 Ign-F1 despite 2.96$\\times$ fewer examples; CED filtering is insensitive to NLI scorer capacity (+1.10pp with DeBERTa-v3-large vs.\\ v3-base, within seed noise; Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error patterns.}\nRSFT reduces truncation FN, hallucinated FP, and duplicates by 6--22\\% across scales, but evidence quality is unchanged ($\\Delta < 0.4$pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_analysis}). RSFT improves \\emph{what} the model extracts but not \\emph{how well} it grounds extractions.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nThree guidelines for evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity (lower observed variance across Qwen3 and LLaMA, though partly confounded by shared SFT initialization; $N{=}3$ seeds per configuration); (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both extraction accuracy and evidence quality.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{EDCR as a diagnostic.}\nEDCR (\\S\\ref{sec:edcr}) correlates with extraction quality at two levels: cross-method ($\\rho = -0.89$, $n = 7$, $p = 0.007$; Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum}; $\\rho = -0.83$, $n = 6$, $p = 0.042$ excluding the GRPO QLoRA outlier) and per-relation ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$, $p < 0.001$), the latter providing validation independent of method-level sample size and orthogonal to Evi-F1 ($\\rho = -0.06$, $p = 0.58$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). EDCR shares TP in both numerator and denominator with Rel-F1, contributing to the observed negative correlation; partial correlations controlling for Evi-Recall$_\\text{TP}$ are virtually unchanged ($\\rho = -0.8541$ vs.\\ $-0.8554$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). RSFT avoids decoupling because discrete output selection cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off (Appendix~\\ref{sec:rsft_theory}). CED operates effectively at the response level but fails at sentence-level granularity (Evi-F1 $-$7.7pp), as filtering individual sentences disrupts co-reference chains.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation.}\nCED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT performance (Rel-F1 45.63 vs.\\ 45.61); stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ by 1.02 Ign-F1 despite 2.96$\\times$ fewer examples; CED filtering is insensitive to NLI scorer capacity (+1.10pp with DeBERTa-v3-large vs.\\ v3-base, within seed noise; Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error patterns.}\nRSFT reduces truncation FN, hallucinated FP, and duplicates by 6--22\\% across scales, but evidence quality is unchanged ($\\Delta < 0.4$pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_analysis}). RSFT improves \\emph{what} the model extracts but not \\emph{how well} it grounds extractions. Appendix~\\ref{sec:qualitative} presents representative examples illustrating evidence precision differences, GRPO format gaming under collapse, and per-relation EDCR diagnostics.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nThree guidelines for evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity (lower observed variance across Qwen3 and LLaMA, though partly confounded by shared SFT initialization; $N{=}3$ seeds per configuration); (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both extraction accuracy and evidence quality.\n",
